Also on the branch: the check says a model's parts with no shape once a run. My gate: type check, 1,058 tests (none fail in a bare checkout), ratchet 740 against 740 (no record changes; nothing the check computes imports the changed files), and open-all on 5174 with the merge in: 190 of 190 models open, every model draws all its parts as parts but the 42120 (716 of 968, the 252 segments with their reason). The gate waited an hour and a half: the pool was full by its claims (the sweep's three browsers 9,000 MB, a full check the rest), and each time a check's child ended the check took the room at once, so a larger request never got in. Decided: the oldest waiter goes first (claude/pool-oldest-first, gated next); the solver owner held its slow sets so that the gate could run when its full check ended.
254. **The pool: the oldest waiter goes first. Merged.** claude/pool-oldest-first ef3b33b4. A request that must wait writes a waiting entry with its time and size in the pool's folder; no request, of a check or of the wrapper, takes room while an older one still waits; a waiter whose process is gone is dropped. Gate: type check, 1,061 tests (three new: an older large waiter holds back a younger small one that would fit; a dead waiter holds back no one; a check's reserve waits in turn), none fail in a bare checkout; the check has one caller of the pool (reserve), which now waits in turn. Only processes started from the new code take turns, and an older process counts a waiting entry as room in use, which errs on the safe side. So every session merges preview before its next check or wrapped run.
255. **The pool does not see a browser's memory; claims for browsers by the part count until it does.** The worker found it when I asked for the sweep browsers' peaks: Playwright's chromium makes a process group of its own, and the wrapper adds up only the wrapped command's group, so it printed 291 MB for a sweep of the 8032 and 347 MB for my own gate drags, which I had taken as true. Measured by the worker's own sampler: chromium 2,752 MB for the 8110, 1,558 MB for the 9397, 1,427 MB for the 42078. So the claims of 3,000 MB a browser were about right for the largest models and too high for the rest. Decided: the solver owner mends the measure on its next branch (the wrapped process with all its descendants by parent, whatever their group; the same for stopping; a test). Until the pool measures it, a browser claims 1,000 MB plus 0.85 MB a part, the worker's fit, in the sweep, in open-all.ts and in the drag scripts.
256. **Route 5 is merged: a solver step takes about half the time on the 9397, no result changes, and the 9395's selector lever is freed. Two collision stops are in the check only, which is traced next.** claude/step-time e7142119 (decision 241's route; held by decision 243 until its full check). The full check and the slow sets: 740 functions work, as before; 1 better (the 42020's function 2, its wheels now come back), 0 worse; no result changes; one cause changes (the 9395's function 5, from first-failed to not-driven, because the lever now goes). Time: in the check a 0.1 rad piece of the 9397's steering takes 1,006 ms, not 1,880, and of the 8110's crane slew 5,423 ms, not 8,169; the 42100's function 7 is unchanged; the 8110's slow check takes 17,078 s, not 23,400. My gate: type check; the test suite (1,063 pass); the ratchet (740 against 740, 1 better, 0 worse); the 9395 and the 42020 checked by name give the stored records but for times; and the same turns in the viewer on port 5174 before and after: the 9397's steering knob takes 281 to 289 ms a step, not 505 to 536, and the 9395's lever p612 goes the 0.3 rad asked, where before it stuck at 0.067 rad with no collision. Three things it shows, all ordered or noted. (a) The check stops the 9395's winch (function 4) at 1.17 rad, p742 against p773, and the 42083's steering at -1.47 rad, p1106 against p1126; the viewer turns both the full 1.5 rad (the winch seen in my gate too). The check and the viewer must agree, so the solver owner traces these two before anything else, and the one that is wrong is mended. (b) On preview the check called the 9395's function 4 works with its first step, the lever, gone 0.07 of 0.3 rad: a function whose first step does not go its distance must not pass. The solver owner finds why it did and how many working functions have a first step that fell short. (c) The same free motions in another basis give other numbers on some functions that already did not come back: the 42052's function 4 is left 41.47 LDU out of place, not 2.28, and the 9397's function 1 2.98 degrees, not 0.98; the 8110's functions 9 and 10 and the 42066's function 2 come closer. So what follows the free motions depends on the basis they are given in, which it should not; noted for the trace of the 42100's loops and the shrink. Route 5 is not the whole cure for the slow viewer: 281 ms a step is still three to four pictures a second while the 9397 is steered, and the 8110 is far slower; the solver in a worker of its own stays in the queue.
257. **Erik's rule of 2026-11-20: a function too slow in the viewer to tell whether it works is not a working function. Speed work comes in two classes, and the halting class goes first.** His words: functions that are slow but functional are worked on later; functions so slow that it is hard to discern whether they are functional must be mended before they can be called functional; after the current work, everything is tested again and the halting ones mended, which also makes later testing faster. I agree and order it so. (a) The measure is the viewer's time for one solver step of a control's turn, which the sweep already records for every working function. (b) The line between the two classes is Erik's to set, by feel: at about 520 ms a step he could not tell that the 9397's steering worked; whether about 285 ms is enough is asked of him. No function is marked by the line before he sets it. (c) A working function over the line is held at partly in `data/holds.json` with the reason too slow to tell and its ms a step, and comes back by itself when a later timing puts it under the line. So the count of working functions will fall. A rough scale from the check's own records (seconds of check per radian turned; the viewer's steps are about two thirds of that): 85 of 737 working functions take over 10 s a radian and 31 over 30 s, against the 9397's steering at 12; the worst are on the 42078, the 42082, the 8110, the 42100 and the 9397. (d) The worker's trial shows that the sweep's own time is not the solver's: of the 9397's 12,522 s only about 1,500 s were turns, the rest opening the model 18 times, waits for the collision test and the camera. The worker stops the trial's two runs still going (the 8110, the 42078), makes the sweep open a model once a set, and then runs the part that turns each control (A) over every working function of every set first: that gives shown or not shown by turn and the ms a step of all, which is the worklist. The part by pointer (B) follows. (e) The solver owner, after the two short tasks of decision 256, works on speed before anything else, the slowest first from that worklist: the trace of the 42100's loops (13 rounds a step), the solver in a worker of its own so the page stays live, then further routes for the step itself. The 8110's six rows and the shrink's weakness come after. (f) After each speed mend only the functions over the line are timed again, which is quick. Main is still not offered before the sweep has run and its findings are mended or marked.
258. **Erik's answer of 2026-11-20 on the 9397: still too slow, about 2 seconds for each turn of the knob's motion, and a part turns with the steering that should not. My figure of 285 ms was the wrong measure.** (a) I had timed one long turn of 1.5 rad, 43 solver steps in one call, and divided. A drag or Auto spin makes many small calls, and each call costs about 900 ms before its first step: in the viewer on port 5174 one call of one step (0.035 rad) of the knob's shaft takes 1,360 to 1,386 ms, and a call of eight steps 467 ms a step. So the measure of decision 257 is the time of one call of one step, as a drag makes it, not the time a step in a long turn. The sweep records both. (b) The line: Erik could not judge at 1,360 ms and more, so a working function at or over that is too slow to tell and is held at partly. Where the line lies below that is asked again when a mend has made the 9397 faster; functions between are not marked until then. (c) The first speed task of the solver owner is now what each call does before its first step, which no route so far touched. (d) The part: p1286 (918.dat, a battery box lid, with no connection points of its own in the library) is joined by a rigid connection at its own origin to p1001, the 2-long axle of the steering shaft, 20 LDU from it, so it swings round the shaft; its twin p1285 is joined the same way to a beam of the frame. The worker traces the rule that makes these joins and counts, over all 190 models, the parts so joined to a body that moves; a mend that can change a result gets decision 96's check. (e) Erik's order of the same day: the sanity check's output is a table of every function with the time for each step. The sweep's part A writes it, for every working function, and I put the time beside each function on the Technic status page.
259. **A loose part rides with a part it touches as built; the worker builds it, with a full check.** The worker's trace of the 9397's lid (decision 258): the rule is rideAlong, which welds a part that no rule attached, at its own origin, to the part whose connection point starts nearest that origin. A lid's origin is not where it sits and a connection point's start is not a part's shape, so the nearest can be a shaft that passes by: the lid p1286 went to the steering shaft's axle at 22.9 LDU, the battery box's switch p1287 to a part of another moving body, and the other lid p1285 to the case's body by luck. Over the 190 models rideAlong makes 2,842 welds; measured against what each loose part touches as built, 217 welds in 52 sets go to a body that none of the touched parts is on (most on the 42112, the 42069, the 42110, the 8274, the 42055 and the 42099). The mend: a loose part or group that touches parts which are not loose rides with one of those, the one whose connection point is nearest, as now among all; a part that touches nothing keeps today's rule. Not the frame: the 9397's case is itself in a body that is not the frame. The contact is worked out in the inference itself, from the shapes of the loose parts and their neighbours, by the same test as the collision pass uses for parts that touch as built; the kept collision files are a cache and must not decide bodies. To report with it: how many loose parts touch parts of more than one body (where the nearest point still chooses), the time it adds to opening a large model, and which kept pivot and collision files it makes stale. It is general work: one rule, 52 sets. How it is built (the worker's question of the same day): the inference has no shapes and runs before the viewer reads them, so inferMechanism gets an input, for each loose part the parts it touches as built, made by one shared function from the meshes of the loose parts and their box neighbours through the collision pass's own test; the viewer and the check both make it before the mechanism is built. Not the other way, the mechanism worked out a second time once the shapes are in: the bodies would change after the model opens. Every caller that gives results passes the input; only hand-built test models go without. The first build's full check (claude/ride-with-touched 86addadc, not merged): 739 work against 740, none better and three worse, all where a loose part or group touches more than one body and the nearest connection point among the touched then chooses: the 42084's carried second model went onto a gear of the hook arm's drive and locked the arm, the 42069's hoses onto a door's hinge instead of the frame, a chain link of the 42107 onto the other of two bodies. 220 loose parts in 67 models touch more than one body, as feared when the rule was ordered. So the rule is narrowed to what is certain: a loose part or group whose touched parts are all of one body rides with that body (the 9397's lids and switch, which touch only the battery box's case); one that touches parts of several bodies, or none, keeps today's rule. Which of several bodies a part belongs to (the one it rests on most, or neither where it is a hose from one to the other) is a question for another day, with the list of those 220 in hand. The narrowed rule gets its own full check, slow sets too, and must come out with none worse.
260. **Erik's priorities of 2026-11-20, and a blind trial on models the check has never seen.** His words in short: the per-set work of finding file errors and writing function lists is very worthwhile, because it builds a next OMR database with better accuracy and more data than an assemblage of parts; but the long tail must not bog us down at the expense of overall performance, of where we truly stand with the green sets, and of sets we have not tried yet. So the order of attention is: (1) speed in the viewer (the solver owner, decision 258); (2) the true state of the working functions (the worker's sweep and its table, decisions 252 and 257); (3) general rules that many sets share (decision 259's kind) and the blind trial; (4) the tail of single sets, which goes on as the product it is (corrections and lists are kept as data for a later report to the OMR) but only where it does not hold up 1 to 3. A session that finds itself on a fault only one set has says so and parks it unless it is cheap. The blind trial, to measure whether our progress carries to a model we never looked at: all 186 sets have been in the check since 2026-10-24 (446 of 1,040 checked functions worked then, 42%; 740 of 1,045 now, 70%), so nothing so far measures it. The OMR lists 1,470 sets today (read 2026-11-20; the listing is in the notes folder, blind-trial/omr-listing-2026-11-20.json); its 183 Technic sets are all in the check, so the unseen models are of other themes: Model Team (6), Creator Expert and Icons vehicles, the larger Racers, Creator sets with functions. They are built more of ordinary bricks, so the trial tests a wider claim than another Technic set would. Steps: (a) the worker lists the candidates, the OMR sets outside Technic and trains whose description by LEGO or a review names at least two functions worked by a mechanism, without opening any model; (b) I draw ten by a recorded random seed; (c) Erik says yes to the download of those ten files and their booklets, by name and size, and I download them; (d) the lists are written from LEGO's pages and reviews and committed before any model is opened or checked; (e) one run: every model opened in the viewer, one check, the time of one call for each function; nothing is mended and the figures stay apart from the 186's; (f) the report: the share of functions that work at the first pass beside 42% and 70%, and each failure sorted as general (a rule, a part's data, the solver) or the model's own (a fault in its file, its list). About ten models give some 50 to 80 functions, so the share is rough, some 12 points either way. After the trial the ten join the suite, and a later trial needs new models. Technic sets with no OMR file (364 of the 547 in our table) would be the closer test; their files exist only on unofficial sites, which I do not download from, so they join only if Erik supplies files. The trial's steps (a) and (d) take the worker about a day and come after part A's table and the mend of decision 259.
261. **Every time measured so far was taken on a loaded machine; the figures stand as ratios, not as times. The slowest functions are timed again with the pool to themselves.** Erik saw on 2026-11-20 that he had judged the viewer's speed while all cores were busy, and tests on another machine from now on. Looked at then: a load average of 38 on 12 threads, with checks and sweeps in the pool, which limits memory and processes but not processor time. So: (a) before-and-after pairs measured at the same moment hold as ratios (route 5's halving; one call of one step against a call of eight, which showed about 900 ms a call); (b) the absolute figures do not: my 1,360 ms for one small turn of the 9397's steering, the sweep's table, and Erik's own 2 seconds are all too high by a factor not known; (c) the line of decision 258 at 1,360 ms is withdrawn until Erik has judged on a quiet machine, and no function is held for speed before that; (d) the sweep writes the machine's load average into each set's record, and the status page says that its times were taken under load; (e) after part A, every function over 250 ms for one small turn is timed again alone, through the pool's wrapper asking for the whole pool (16,000 MB), so that nothing else runs, and those figures replace the loaded ones, marked as quiet; (f) the solver owner's speed figures are pairs taken at the same moment or taken with the pool to itself. A limit on processor time in the pool is not built now: the checks' results do not depend on it.
262. **The two stops in the check only are explained: one was a linkage flipped, now refused; the other is a true contact at the end of travel. The check and the viewer choose their holds at other moments, which is looked at with the speed work.** The solver owner's trace (decision 256's tasks), claude/check-stops 669224e7. (a) The 9395's winch (function 4): its loops are jammed, so it turns on play; a step just after one on play was excused from the stray test, and one such step closed the loops by flipping a linkage (a joint 27.7 rad off its way, 506 parts moved up to 140 LDU), which only the collision test refused, naming a pair that met in the flipped pose. Mend: PLAY_STRAY, a quarter turn; a step that leaves a joint further off its way than that is refused, on play or not. Over 20 sets with play, 30 of 32 excused steps strayed at most 0.73 rad and the 9395's two 25.5 and 27.7, so the bound lies between two kinds 35 times apart and is tuned to no result. Decision 96's proof: only steps on or just after play can change, and every set with play was checked by name (20 sets whole, the 9 play functions of 8 more); only the 9395's function 4 differs (its collision stop becomes an end of travel at the same 1.17 rad; it still counts as works). (b) The 42083's steering stop at -1.47 rad is a round tile against a beam, 0.5 LDU of overlap that the shrink leaves as 0.0023 LDU; the check's pose and the viewer's differ by 0.001 LDU there, 0.02 rad before the end of travel. No mend; the result is the same. (c) The false green: a first step that falls short only adds a line and a cause, and the result comes from what moved; the 9395's function 4 was the one working function that ever had it, and none has it today. Ordered all the same: a function whose first step does not go its distance is at most partly. (d) Found on the way: the viewer's turnPart chooses what to hold afresh at each call, the check once for a whole way, and the viewer took the 9395's winch to 0.12, 0.2 or 1.5 rad by the step size. A result must not depend on the step size, and the check must turn a control as a drag does; the solver owner first says how a pointer drag, Auto spin, turnPart and the check each choose, and whether that choosing is the 900 ms a call of decision 258. Until then the 9395's winch is a doubtful green for the sweep to judge.
263. **Where a small turn's time goes on the 9397, the first mend for it merged, and the next route: the same arithmetic made cheaper, results bit for bit the same.** The solver owner's measure (loaded machine, so ratios): choosing what to hold 429 ms (two workings-out of a joint's motion), the first step 444 ms (two more), each further step about 228 ms (one), the collision test 14 ms a step, and 333 to 369 ms after each move for the Stats card's readout, whose turning circle worked the free motions out afresh on every move of a car, card closed or not. A pointer drag chooses its holds once, at pointer-down, as the check does once for a way, and then pays one step a frame; Auto spin keeps its holds while it runs; only turnPart (the address's turn=, the sweep, longer-turn) chooses at every call. So decision 262's worry is answered: the check turns a control as a drag does, and the 9395's winch going to other angles by step size came from turnPart called in pieces. And the sweep's two times mean: one small turn is the first move of a drag (holds and first step), a step in a long turn is each further frame of it. Merged, claude/call-cost 29c7df48: the readout is worked out only while the Stats card is open, a car's turning circle only once the model has stood still 0.3 s, and frameTurningRadius puts back what it overwrote between a drag's steps (scrubNeeded, leastScrub, slipRows). The check calls neither, so no result can change. My gate on port 5174, before then after, five calls of one step each: the steering shaft 1,079 to 1,201 ms before (load 32), 818 to 913 after (load 24); the truck rolled 3,008 to 3,485 before, 2,669 to 2,711 after; the solver owner's pairs taken at the same moment say the same (1,180 to 880, 3,600 to 2,970). The Stats card opened shows the turning circle. Type check and 1,063 tests pass. What is left is the solver's own, about 60% in chooseMotion's leastTurning (Gram matrices and their eigenvalues) and 30% in freeMotionsApart. Routes offered: (a) the same arithmetic cheaper, (b) other numerics, (c) the solver in a worker. Decided: (a) now, and not timidly: the dense kernels on flat typed arrays with the order of operations kept, and exact zeros skipped (adding a product with an exact zero changes no sum), which is where arrays of arrays of numbers lose most; each kernel proven bit for bit against the old one by a test on real matrices of the 9397, the 8110 and the 42100, and one full check at the end of the route, whose records must be the same but for times. Then (c)'s design as a note. (b) only on my word, after (a)'s figures, since results may move.
264. **The check: a function whose control worked first does not go its distance is at most partly. Merged.** claude/first-short 63d87ff7, decision 262's order. Before, the short first step only named the cause of a function that failed anyway, and the result came from what moved, so the 9395's winch had passed with its selector lever at 0.07 of 0.3 rad. Short is what the check already judged when it wrote the line, with no new tolerance: a turned control under 0.9 of its `by`, a slid one under 0.9 of it or not able to slide, a driving ring not shifted to another click-in place. Decision 96's proof: only a function with such a line can change, and in the stored records only the 42024's functions 3 and 4 have one, both partly with the cause first-failed already; the solver owner checked the 42024 and the 9395 whole on the change and they agree with their records. My gate: the change read, type check, 1,063 tests. No result changes.
265. **The speed route after the kernels: sparse rows, then each step worked block by block, the dragged joint's block alone choosing the motion.** Route (a) so far (claude/dense-kernels, its full check running since 15:15 on 2026-11-20) takes about 30% off a step with the same bits; its profile is now flat, so no kernel gives the ten times the large models need. The solver owner measured what one step turns: the 42083's steering 16 of 462 tree joints (23 of 358 bodies moved), the 9397's steering 14 of 695, the 9397 rolled 15 of 695 (every body moves, but rigidly), the 42100's function 7 121 of 463. The dense work grows with the cube of the free motions' count, so a step worked on a few percent of the mechanism is tens of milliseconds, not seconds. It cannot be bit for bit the same (the still part is kept still by the choice of mix, not by rows; pivots are compared across all rows; the full calculation leaves noise of 1e-17 where zeros should be), so it is a route that may move results, with a full check. Decided, in two steps. (1) Sparse rows, exact: the equation rows as places and values from where they are written (the constraint writers, inBasis, buildEquations, nullSpace), which removes the dense rows of every coordinate that each round builds and is the ground for step 2; merged under the kernels' proof (old against new by Object.is, end to end the same bits) and a full check. (2) Blocks, to rounding: the coordinates fall into blocks that no row joins (rows as the solver sees them at that step: the equations, the tyres' slip, the springs, the frame); the null space and its orthonormalizing are worked block by block, and the choice of motion and the closing of loops work on the dragged joint's block alone, the other blocks keeping still as least motion leaves them now. This is route 5's idea for whole blocks, and it takes the moving part from the rows themselves, not from a guess of what will move. A guess beyond that (holding still what the rows do not hold) is not built without my word. Before step 2 is built: the sizes of the blocks on the four cases above, counted from today's rows, since slip rows may join a car's steering to its frame and drive; if the dragged block is most of the car, step 2 gives little and the plan changes. Step 2's proof: a compare mode that works a step both ways and gives the largest difference in any pose and joint angle, run over the check's turns on some twenty sets and to be within rounding; then the full check with the ratchet and every record that differs.
266. **Blocks do not pay; the second step is a working set: after a drag's first, full step, later steps are worked on the joints that step turned and their neighbours, the rest held.** The count ordered in decision 265: the rows tie almost every coordinate into one block (the 9397's steering 416 of 451 coordinates and 169 of 204 free motions; the 42083's 468 of 488; the 42100's fn7 378 of 410; the 8110's crane 670 of 726), the rest being single coordinates that route 5 already sets apart. So decision 265's step 2 by blocks is dropped. Yet a step turns few tree joints: 9 of 421 for the 9397's steering, 1 for a wheel rolled, 12 of 332 for the 42083's steering, 15 of 648 for the 8110's crane, 100 of 386 for the 42100's fn7; the least-motion tiers choose that small part inside a block where the rest could move. Decided, after the sparse rows (step 1, exact, nearly done): (a) a drag's first step is worked in full, as now, when its holds are chosen; (b) its working set is the tree coordinates that step turned, above the noise the full calculation leaves and far below any real rate, with the frame's six if they moved, and one ring more: every coordinate that some row ties directly to one of those; (c) each later step of the same drag, and of Auto spin while it keeps its holds, is worked with every other tree coordinate fixed, its column left out, so the null space, the tiers and the closing of loops run on tens of coordinates, not hundreds; a motion so found is always one the whole mechanism allows, because every row that touches a free coordinate is kept; (d) the set grows when a step turns a coordinate of the ring (its neighbours join), and a step that the small set refuses, jams or stops at a limit is worked again in full and the set made anew, so that the small set never ends a motion that the whole mechanism would go on with; (e) the check turns its controls through the same code, so it and the viewer agree. Why this and not the solver owner's first proposal of following the ties from the dragged joint: the ties reach everything, and the set must come from what the solver itself chose, at the same moment at which a drag already fixes its other choices (its holds, whether it uses the suspension, whether it spares the tyres). What can differ from today, beyond rounding: the full calculation might, some steps into a drag, start to move a far part that the set holds; (d) covers the cases with a cause the set can see, and the proof must find any other. Proof: a compare mode that works each step both ways and gives the largest difference in any pose and joint angle and whether the two stopped at the same place, run over the check's turns on some twenty sets chosen for their kinds (steering on the ground, a gearbox with a selector, a crane with catches, linkages, tracks, a function on play); then the full check with the ratchet and every record that differs. The solver owner writes a short note of the design and its failure cases before building, and builds it behind an option so that both ways stay runnable. A drag's first move stays as slow as now; what it buys is every frame after it. The solver owner's design note (working-set.md in the notes folder, same day) and my word on it: built behind the option `workingSet`; turned means over 1e-9 of the dragged joint's turn, the solver's own zero, six orders over the full calculation's noise and three under the smallest real rate in the records; the set is closed per body; a stop of any kind (refusal, jam, limit, collision, dead point), a ring coordinate turned, or a gap only a fixed coordinate could close sends the step to the full calculation, so a stop is always judged in full. A far part can start to move unannounced in three ways: a tie or near-tie within a tier (a differential's other side, a free pin taking the turn), tyres going from scrubbing to rolling, and a step going on play. No cheap certificate exists, since the tiers' optimum needs the full null space. So: no working set while a drag is on play, while tyres are near their scrub limit, or for a car on the ground whose frame is not in the set; and a full step every so far of the dragged joint's turn as an audit is built as a setting, off or an interval, not as a fixed part, because one full step in seven caps the gain at seven times and stalls the drag at each. The compare mode is run first with the audit off, to count how often and in which kinds the small set and the full calculation part; I set the audit's default, or a cheaper signal for the kind found, from that count. A drag that keeps the mix it chose first may also be the steadier answer where the full calculation changes mix at a tie; the ratchet and the compare mode's cases decide that, not the bits. First results (the solver owner, same day; claude/working-set on top of sparse-rows): the 9397's steering takes 15 to 19 ms a step on a set of 39 columns, where the full step takes about 200, and the two agree to 3e-15; the 42100's function 7 runs on play, so it stays in full and gains nothing; on the 42083's steering (88 columns) the two part, by 9.5e-4 LDU after 0.45 rad and growing: the full calculation turns the front suspension arm's hinge a little more each step (bump steer, about 3e-6 rad a step by then), a rate that is nothing where the drag starts, so the arm is outside the set and beyond the ring, and on the set the tie rod's ball end takes up the difference; both close the loops. Two rings would catch it but make the set 474 of 488 columns. Decided: the working set is an approximation with a stated bound, not a calculation to rounding. Its bound is the play the solver itself allows a joint, PLAY, 0.1 LDU (for an angle, that at a stud's distance, 0.005 rad): over a whole turn of a function the two ways may part by less than that in any pose, they must stop at the same place for the same reason, and the check's records must come out with the same results. A real set's parts have more play than that, and a difference a tenth of what the check counts as moved cannot change what a user sees. A case over the bound is looked at one by one; the audit setting or a wider set is for those.
267. **The sweep's first pass is through all 740 working functions: 566 are shown by the turn, 144 are not, 30 are not decided. Most of the 144 are not yet known to be real; sorting them is the worker's first task, and the quiet pass waits for the new solver code.** Part A (claude/viewer-sweep f06e2682, records and data/viewer/table.csv merged into preview): 184 sets in 4 h 41 min, all on the loaded machine. Of the 57 sets where every checked function works, 32 have every function shown and 25 have one or more not shown or not decided. The 144 by what the record says: in 106 the listed parts did not move, in 19 a part listed to stay moved, in 13 a collision stops a control (9 that should turn without end, 4 with a small travel), in 6 the sweep could not do the function's first step. Of the 106, in 72 the sweep's turn of the control went nowhere (in 28 of those the check had freed pins held by friction, which part A does not do; the rest are controls that the viewer's turnPart does not turn, a slide or a part that is no knob, or real faults), in 9 it went part of the way, and in 35 it went all the way and the listed parts stayed (16 of them with first steps). So the count holds the sweep's own limits and real faults together. The 30 not decided are controls with a travel of 0.2 rad or less that a collision stops, which the booklet decides (decision 252). Ordered: (a) part A frees pins where the check freed them, as a user does with Shift, and runs again on those functions; (b) part B, the pointer, runs on every function part A does not show, since a drag can do what turnPart cannot; a function shown by neither is the viewer's fault and is held at partly (decision 252's holds), one shown by the pointer only is shown; (c) for the 35 where the control turns and the parts stay, the worker compares the check's turn with the viewer's on five of them, step by step, and says what differs in kind before anything is held; (d) the 19 with a part listed to stay that moved are looked at the same way on three. The quiet pass (95 functions of 250 ms or more for one small turn, with the pool to itself) is taken out of the queue: it would hold up two full checks, and its figures would be of code that the sparse rows and the working set replace within days; it runs once on that new code, on my word. The slowest of part A, loaded: the 8110's functions 1, 3, 4, 13 and 2 (17.7, 11.7, 11.5, 9.6 and 6.3 s for one small turn), the 42082's function 2 (4.4 s), the 9397 rolled (3.3 s).
268. **The exact speed work is merged: the kernels of decision 263 and the sparse rows of decision 265's first step.** claude/sparse-rows 904f7671. The solver's sums go over the places that are not zero, and the equation rows are kept as places and values from where the constraints write them; every new kernel gives the old one's numbers to the last bit (tests/kernels.test.ts, old against new by Object.is on real steps of the 9397, the 8110 and the 42100 and on random inputs with -0, subnormals, infinities and NaN). Its full check with the slow sets: all 187 set files the same as preview's but for times, which I compared myself; 740 work, none better or worse; 778 collision stops the same. The solver owner's pairs taken at the same moment: the 42100's function 7 one call of one step 1,640 to 492 ms and four steps 4,455 to 1,262 ms; the 9397's steering 860 to 590 ms; the 9397 rolled 12,193 to 8,592 ms for four steps; the 42083's steering 3,300 to 2,190 ms. The sets' check times add up to 0.74 of preview's. My gate: the records' comparison, the ratchet, the type check; the merged tree is the very tree the solver owner tested (1,068 tests pass), so I did not run the suite again, and my own timings after the merge I dropped when they stood behind twenty others in the pool (see decision 269).
269. **We are test-bound; the rules of proof change so that a branch no longer costs a full check.** Erik's question of 2026-11-20: three days with the status graph flat and every core busy day and night. The reasons, plainly: since 2026-11-19 the work went to things that do not raise the count (the memory pool, the viewer opening every model, the sweep, speed), by his own redirect and rightly; and every change to the solver or the inference cost a full check of two to three hours on a full machine, by each session for each branch, with the slow sets and my gate's runs on top. On 2026-11-20 alone: route 5's check, the kernels' check (stopped at 9%), the sparse rows' check, the ride-along rule's check twice, the sweep's five hours, and the reruns. The machine can do about four full checks a day, and we asked for more than that. Changed, from now: (a) A branch's proof is aimed, not full. For a change to the inference: the digest before and after names the sets whose mechanism changes, and those are checked by name; the others cannot change. For a change to the solver that claims the same bits: the kernel tests and an end-to-end comparison of bits; no check. For a change to the solver that may move results: a standing sample of about twenty sets of different kinds, the solver owner's list for the compare mode, kept in the repository, plus the sets the change is about. For a change to the check or the sweep: the functions that the stored records show can change. (b) One full check of preview a day at most, started by me when a day's merges are in, not by a timer; it is the net under (a). A function it finds worse is traced by name to its merge and mended or taken out the next day. The slow sets run only before an offer of main. (c) My gate does not run again what a session ran on the same tree: I compare records, run the ratchet and the type check, and try in the viewer only what changes what the viewer shows. (d) No timing or sweep on code that is about to be replaced. (e) Speed comes first also for this reason: the check's own time falls with the solver's (0.74 with decision 268; the working set next). What this risks: preview can carry a regression for a day that an aimed proof missed; main is not offered without a full check and the 8110's slow check, so it cannot reach main. Decision 96 is narrowed by this, not dropped: its proof that only named functions can change is now the usual way, and the full check the daily net.
270. **The narrowed rule for loose parts is merged: a loose part or group rides with the body it touches as built when all it touches is of one body.** claude/ride-with-touched a547cb8c (decision 259). 301 of 2,842 welds change in 55 models, 134 of them to another body; of the 217 welds the trace called wrong it mends 116 and leaves 101 (a group with one part touching a second body keeps today's rule). The 9397's lids and switch are in the battery box's case's body; steered 0.6 rad in the viewer on port 5174 they do not move against the case, and the wheels still steer. The worker's check, every set but the 8110: 740 work, none better, none worse; no new collision stop, one gone (the 8257's function 7). The digest differs for 63 of 190 models, and every function that differs is in one of them; ten sets' records differ in substance and are taken from the branch, the rest stay preview's. What it shows that was hidden: the 8466's wheel p676, listed to turn in functions 3 and 5, now stays, because it rides with its spindle, the right body, and no joint of wheel on spindle is read; the weld to the other wheel had hidden that (both functions were partly and stay so). The 42066's function 2 comes back worse (partly before and after). The 8110's digest differs in connections only, its two changed welds staying in their bodies, so its slow check is not run for this. My gate: the type check, 1,074 tests, every model opened in the viewer, the ratchet. Two things for the record: the model loader now waits for the shapes (loadModel is async), which the kernels test of decision 268 had to follow; and the test suite now takes about 7,400 MB at its peak, not 4,900, since the set tests read shapes. Open: the loose parts that touch several bodies (220 in 67 models), and the 8466's wheel on its spindle, both parked behind the sweep's sorting.
271. **Our viewer tests brought the web copy of the parts library to refuse everyone; they take the parts from the local library now.** The viewer packs a model opened by file from an LDraw library on the web (erik-larsen.github.io/ldraw) and keeps the parts in the browser; a test's browser with no window keeps nothing, so every model opened fetched each of its parts again. After a day of sweeps, on the evening of 2026-11-20, GitHub answered 429, too many requests, for about a quarter of an hour: 80 of 190 models failed to open in my gate, and anyone opening a model of their own on the public site, or Erik on his other machine, would have met the same. Mended in preview d31f722d: scripts/drags/drag.ts adds `library=/ldraw/` to the viewer's address (the ldraw/ folder that the viewer's own server serves), and open-all.ts and sweep.ts use it; KINEBRICK_WEB_LIBRARY=1 turns it off on purpose. With the local library the 80 models open in under ten minutes, so loading was also a large part of the sweep's time. Sweep records made while the web copy refused are void and run again; part A's first pass ended at 17:10, before it, and stands. A rule for every session: no test opens a model from the web library.
272. **The working set is merged and on by default, with no audit.** The solver owner's shadow count on the standing sample (`data/check-sample.csv`, 19 sets of different kinds, 138 functions, 3,421 turns; branch claude/working-set 04a97098) worked every drag twice, on the set and in full, each from its own last state. Facts: (a) no function changes its result in any of the 19 sets; (b) 2,746 turns (80.3%) ran on the set; the rest fell back (play 107, short 35, ring 25, new holds 0) or were excluded (157); (c) five functions come apart by more than PLAY (0.1 LDU or 0.005 rad). One is a stop: the 42083's steering stops in full at -1.47 rad on the pair p1106 and p1126, whose contact is 0.0023 LDU, and on the set it turns to -1.5 rad. Four are free-wheeling functions (42078 fn2, fn3, fn13; 42125 fn8) where the full calculation lets parts stray that the function should not move (half bushes, universal-joint halves, crankshaft parts) and the set keeps them still; the parts come back better on the set in three of the four and worse in one (42078 fn2: 0.71 against 0.92 LDU). Everything else is under 7e-3 LDU, most under 1e-7. Decisions: (1) the set's motion is taken in all five. A user expects the steering to turn to its end, as the viewer showed before the set, and expects wheels that turn free to move nothing else; the full calculation's stop rests on a contact at the edge of what the collision test sees, and its stray parts are a fault of its own, which stays on the solver owner's list. (2) No audit (the setting stays, at 0). An audit is a full step now and then, and it can only bring the full calculation's motion back in; in every case over the bound that motion is the fault. The risk left is a long run on one drag (Auto spin in the viewer), where a difference that still grows at the end of the check's turn (9391 fn5, the tracks: 6.2e-3 LDU) could pass the bound: the solver owner runs two functions for twenty revolutions in the shadow and says which side is off; if it is the set, the audit gets an interval. (3) On by default (`KINEBRICK_WORKING_SET=0` turns it off), because the viewer has no environment to set an option in, and the viewer is where the speed is wanted; the two settings read straight from `process.env` now go through `setting()`, which a browser can run. Only a drag that turns a joint uses the set (`turnJoint`); a push is worked in full as before. Proof at the merge (decision 269: none of the session's runs repeated): the type check; the test suite with the set on, 89 files and 1,074 tests; and the 9397's steering dragged by pointer in the viewer on port 5174, the set on and off one after the other at load 46 to 48: 15 ms a step on the set (most 23) against 120 ms in full (most 211), 66 of 68 calls on the set, no fallback; the first move of the drag takes 243 ms both ways; the joints end within 3.1e-4 rad of each other, the two drags having taken 68 and 60 steps. The check's stored records were made without the set: 42 records of the sample differ in some field (rates at rounding level, six rates where both ways turn the full amount and rounding chooses which is stored, the 42083's stop, the stray lists), and the day's full check of preview writes them. Two things found on the way, both open: the check's choice of the best way (`bestOf`) lets rounding decide a tie, so the solver owner gives it a tie within 1e-9 as its own change, proved on the sample; and the 42078's function 12 takes about 8 times longer since preview's loose-part rule (3,276 s against 415 s, the set on or off alike), whose cause the solver owner finds by a count over the first steps on both bases, not by a pair of whole runs. Merged as 0496df8a.
273. **The viewer sweep's browsers draw in software and take most of the machine.** Looking at what ran during the merge of decision 272: the three browsers of the worker's sweep each had a drawing process at about 340% of a core (`chrome-headless-shell --type=gpu-process`), together about ten of the twelve threads, and the load average stood at 36 to 48 with nothing else heavy running. So much of the load of the last days, under which all times were taken (decision 261) and the checks ran slowly (decision 269), is the sweep drawing every frame without a graphics card. Decision: the worker tries, in this order, the browser with the Mac's graphics card (Playwright's full Chromium, or the headless shell with Metal), and failing that a setting for tests under which the viewer draws only when a test asks for a picture; the proof is the processor share of one browser over the same drag before and after, and the same sweep records. It applies to the next sweep runs, not to the one under way.
274. **What a loose part touches is worked out once per model, as built.** The solver owner's profile of the 42078's function 12 (decision 272) found the cause of its 8 times longer check: the contact test of the loose-part rule (decisions 259 and 270; `touchingFrom` in `src/collisions.ts`) ran afresh at every build of the mechanism, 99 times in that function at 28 s a time, 89% of its 3,115 s. The viewer paid it too: the 42078 took 33 s to read where it took 1.2 s before the rule, and 29 s more after every shift (a gear lever, a lock bar slid), because each shift reads the mechanism again. So the rule that I merged on 20 November made big models with many loose parts slow to open; my gate then opened every model but did not time it. Decisions: (1) the test's answers are kept for the model: the pairs of parts whose boxes meet are found once, each pair's answer is kept once tested, and a pair of two loose parts is not tested at all, because the rule looks only at the parts a loose part touches that are not loose (the 42078's trailer is 1,100 loose parts until it is read as a second model, and nearly all its pairs were of that kind). (2) The test is of the model as built, whatever placement a later reading has. Until now the page tested the parts where they stood after a shift, while its helper for pins used the page's answers as built, so the two could differ; and what a loose part rides with must not change with where the mechanism stands. I made the mend myself in preview, not the worker, because Erik tests in the viewer on 21 November and the worker's folder was held by the sweep. Proof: the type check; every model's mechanism the same to the last bit before and after (`npm run digest`, 190 models); the test suite; and the reading timed on both sides one after the other at load 37 to 40: the 42078 reads in 4.7 s against 33.2 s and reads again in 1.1 s against 28.9 s, the 42110 in 5.8 s against 6.2 s and again in 1.1 s against 1.9 s, the 9397 in 3.2 s both ways and again in 0.24 s against 0.47 s, with the same bodies and loose parts. In the viewer on port 5174 the 42078 opens with the mend in 25 s under that load, with its 296 bodies and 28 loose parts and no error; I took no time in the viewer without the mend. Not proved by these: a reading after a shift, where the placement differs from as built; the day's full check of preview is the net for it. Open, for the worker: the first test of a big model still costs seconds, and kept touches per model, filed like `data/collisions`, would make it free.
275. **The first move of a drag no longer searches twice.** With the working set on (decision 272), a drag's later steps take about 15 ms on the 9397's steering, so its first move is what a user feels. The solver owner's profile (9397 steering, node, load 39): choosing the holds costs about 340 ms, the first step about 300 ms, the second step about 175 ms (still in full: the working set is made from the first two full steps), then about 25 ms a step. The holds and the first step each searched for the turn's motion from the same state with the same holds, the holds twice on the 9397; nearly all the time is that search (`jointMotion`: the null space and least turning's eigen-solve). The mend (claude/first-move c0f5dce4): a search from the same inputs as the last one reuses its free motions and the motions its tiers chose. The inputs are compared exactly, value by value, not by a hash: the joints' angles, every pose, the tyres' travel and scrub, the drag and everything it holds, frozen parts, slides at their ends, the tyres on the ground, the scrub allowed, the working set in use, the options. `KINEBRICK_REUSE_SEARCH=0` turns it off, and `=check` works each reused search out again and counts any that differ. Proof (decision 269: a change that claims the same bits has the kernel tests and bits end to end, no check): the solver owner dragged the first turned control of each of the sample's functions as the pointer does, with the reuse off and on: the same bits of every angle, pose and tyre state in all 19 sets, 89 functions, 469 searches reused, 0 differing in check mode; the first move as pairs in one process at load 39 to 41: 9397 steering 644 ms against 384 ms (holds 328 against 192, first step 316 against 192), 42083 steering 903 against 783 ms, where the holds search only once and most of the first step is outside the search. At the merge I ran the type check, the test suite (89 files, 1,074 tests) and the 9397's steering dragged by pointer in the viewer on port 5174, the reuse on then off at load 41 to 44: the first step 196 ms against 230 ms, 3 searches reused, later steps 16 and 15 ms; my probe does not time the holds, which are chosen at the press. What is left of the first move, in the solver owner's order of gain: the working set's second full step (a cheaper confirmation needs the shadow count again); the null space and the eigen-solve themselves, dense over all joints, which a sparse method would cut for every full step; and the solver in a worker, which keeps the page live whatever a step costs. A push can have a working set as a turn has, by the same wrapper; of the 117 functions the sweep's table shows over 100 ms a step, 116 are turned and one is pushed, but the table hardly times pushes (40 of its 42 pushed functions have no time), so the push's working set waits for the worker's table of the pointer pass. Merged as 60a5dd48.
276. **The check's tie between two ways, and the full check of 21 November.** The function check turns each control both ways and stores the better way's rates (`bestOf`: the way that works, else fewer wrong parts, else the one that went further). Where both ways turn the full reach, 'further' was decided by the last bits of rounding, so the working set, which changes those bits, changed which way's rates were stored in six functions of the sample (decision 272). The mend (claude/bestof-tie e7239cf6, merged as 8fae56f7): two ways that went the same distance within 1e-9 (radians, or LDU for a slide or a push) are a tie, and the tie goes to the first way. This is a tolerance at rounding level, eight orders under what the check counts as moved, not a tuned threshold. Proof on the standing sample, each piece run with the working set on and off: 70 work, 17 partly, 12 no, 11 skipped, 3 not checked both ways, no function changes its result, and the six rates are the same on and off; what still differs between on and off is what decision 272 listed. The tie changes some stored rates on its own where both ways turn the full reach. With it, the day's merges are in (the working set, the touch mend of decision 274, the first-move reuse, the tie), and I start the day's full check of preview on 8fae56f7 in a folder of its own, .claude/worktrees/preview-check, on the branch claude/check-2026-11-21, so that later merges into preview do not change the code under a running check; its records come into preview by a merge of that branch, with the ratchet. The slow sets are left out, as on every day but the one before an offer of main.
277. **The search for a motion is made cheaper in two stages: first with the same bits, then, only after a count, with a different elimination.** After decisions 272 and 275 a full step is the largest cost left: it is every first move, every fallback of the working set, every push, and most of the check's time. The solver owner's count (preview 675ab45f, load 39 to 41): a full step of the 9397's steering is about 200 ms, of which the null space 63 to 77 ms and least turning 63 to 89 ms; of the 42083's steering about 520 ms, of which the null space 250 to 275 ms (13 calls of 1,760 rows by about 460 columns, 1.4% of the places not zero); the holds of the 8110's function 8 take 2,026 ms, of which the null space 1,136 ms. The null space (`nullSpace` in `src/linalg.ts`, an elimination with rook pivoting) scans densely in three places: the search for a column's largest entry, the pass over every row for every pivot, and the copy of its input; on the 42083 those scans are 85% of the places it visits, on the 8110 the updates themselves are most of the work, because eliminating fills zero places in. Decision, stage A, to build now: the null space over the non-zero places only (an index of each column's non-zero rows, kept as places fill in, and the input passed with its non-zero places), with the same pivots and the same arithmetic in the same order, so the same bits. Expected from the count, not yet timed: a full step about 40% shorter on the 42083, 20% on the 9397 and on the 8110's holds. Its proof is that of a change with the same bits (decision 269): the kernel tests with a new test of the new null space against the present one bit for bit, on inputs recorded from the three drags and on random sparse ones, and bits end to end on the sample's drags; no check. Stage B, not to be built yet: an elimination that keeps the fill low (pivots by fewest other entries within a threshold of the largest, columns in the tree's order, elimination below the pivot with back substitution), which moves the last bits and can so move a stop near a dead point, a collision or a tie. The solver owner first counts its gain on the recorded inputs in a harness of its own; I decide from the figures, and its proof would be the standing sample with the 8110, the 42083 and the 9397, every difference classed. Stage C, noted only: the full-rank eigen-solves of least turning (35 to 60 ms of the 9397's 200 ms) and the 658 by 658 solve of a play step on the 8110 (794 ms).
278. **The full check of 21 November: no result moved, and the working set leaves a freely turning part out of place in three functions.** The check of all 185 quick sets at aa3523c8 (the first with the working set of decision 272, the kept touches of decision 274, the first move's reuse of decision 275 and the tie of decision 276) gives the same counts as the stored records: 740 functions work, 214 partly, 91 do not, 178 skipped or not checked. Its records are merged (2628a6e5) as the true state. Summed, the functions took 32,348 s against 39,616 s in the records before, 18% less; both runs shared the machine with other work, so this is a sign and not a measure (decision 261). The biggest gains are the 9397's functions 2 and 6 (1,357 to 719 s, 1,605 to 1,188 s) and the 42083's function 4 (1,111 to 858 s); nothing is slower by more than 4 s. The ratchet marks three functions worse, and all three are the working set's doing, proved by running each with `KINEBRICK_WORKING_SET=0`, which gives the old record: the 42020's function 2 (the wheels roll: two thin beams, parts 125 and 126, end out of place) and the 8274's functions 3 and 6 (pushing the harvester: part 1081, a toothed half bush that turns freely, ends out of place). The results stay as they were (works, partly, works), but each now carries the mark "not back". Four more records differ without a new mark and are the set's too until shown otherwise: the 42066's function 2 comes back torn where it was torn only in a free turn, and turns to -1.92 against -1.96; the 42106's functions 2 and 5 stop at a collision one step earlier (0.53 against 0.57, 0.73 against 0.77); the 8274's function 2 no longer reports the stop at a collision of parts 0 and 153; the 42083's function 1 goes to its limit, as decision 272 foresaw. Fifteen functions are now judged by their first way and not their second (the tie, decision 276), eight piston lists hold the same expected travels in another order between like pistons, and three tyres are named by another of a like pair; none changes a result. **Decided:** the working set stays on, because it makes a held drag about eight times quicker and the faults are a part left out of place in three functions of 1,223, with no result changed; the three marks are accepted in the records, not hidden. The solver owner mends them after stage A of decision 277 and before stage B's count: say why a part outside the set's columns ends out of place (a part that turns freely is most likely worked in the full steps and then left where the full step put it), mend it in the set and not in the check, and account for the four other records. Proof: the seven functions with the set on give the records of the set off, apart from the 42083's limit; the standing sample; no full check.
279. **The sweep's 144 are sorted: 100 are shown after all, 27 are held at partly, and 17 are not settled.** The worker's sort of the 144 working functions that the sweep's first pass did not show (decision 267; claude/viewer-sweep 0db63f96 and 2b89dce5, merged as 9c2c0ccf, the full lists in the notes, viewer-sweep/final144.json). (a) 100 are shown: 59 once the sweep did what a user does (28 with the model lifted or parts held as the list says, 14 with the ring shifted at the drag's end, 11 with Shift where the check freed pins, 6 with the first steps done as the check does them), and 41 by the pointer only, which decision 267 counts as shown. Of those 41, in 37 the control is no knob, so the sweep's one call of turnPart does nothing where a drag works; in 4 turnPart turns the control and the listed parts stay while the pointer moves them (42043 function 10, 42075 function 2, 8063 function 8, 8419 function 3), which is a fault of turnPart to explain, not of what a user sees. (b) 19 are stopped by a collision after a small turn, and the check stops at the same angle and still says works. So the fault is the check's judgment, not a difference between check and viewer: it takes a collision for the end of travel. Ten are parts that turn without end (wheels, tracks, a drive shaft, a power take-off), six are controls whose travel the booklet shows to be long (a boom, a hatch, a door, a mast, a ladder, a bonnet), and three are not decided because their true travel may be small (42004 function 5, 42094 function 5, 42109 function 6). (c) 25 are shown by neither turn nor pointer. In six the control turns and the listed parts stay; in four the control is no knob and the pointer, which sees it, moves no listed part or not all; in one (8844 function 1) parts listed to stay move 3 to 6 LDU. In the other 14 the sweep did not truly try: in three the pointer could not see the control (42078 function 4, 42080 function 1, 8285 function 5), in ten the drag's end seems to undo the first step, a ring sliding back (the 42038's functions 2 and 3, the 42039's 8 and 9, the 42054's 3 and 4, the 42112's 6, the 8110's 8, the 8258's 5, the 9397's 2), and one needs a slide as a first step that the sweep does not do (42078 function 12). **Decided:** by decision 267's rule, 27 functions are held at partly in `data/holds.json`, each with its reason: the 16 a collision stops (ten without end, six with a long travel in the booklet) and the 11 that neither shows where the sweep did try. I tried six of them again myself on today's preview with the sweep (8844 functions 1 and 4, 8835 function 2, 42031 function 2, 42075 function 6, 8285 function 5): all six give the same fault at the same angles, so the working set changed none. The 17 not settled (14 of (c) and the 3 not decided) and the 4 of (a) stay as works until tried properly; they are doubtful greens, named here. The count of working functions falls from 740 to 713 when the check has written the 21 sets' records. Ordered, for the worker, after decision 273's trial: (1) for the 16 collision stops, say for each pair of parts whether the contact is false (parts that overlap as built, a shape too coarse, a part placed wrong in the file) or true, sorted by kind, with a general mend where a kind has one and an erratum offered where the file is wrong; (2) the check judges a stop itself: a function whose part turns without end and is stopped by a collision is at most partly, by the kinds the sweep's judge() already uses, with no new threshold, which replaces ten holds by a rule; proof by the functions whose records have a collision stop; (3) the sweep keeps a first step to the end of the function's drags, does a slide as a first step, and turns the camera or hides parts until the control can be seen, then runs on the 14; (4) the 3 not decided and the 30 of decision 267 by the booklet. A hold goes when the sweep shows the function on a later run.
280. **Stage A of the cheaper search gives the same bits but almost no gain; it stays on its branch until stage B's count.** The solver owner's report (claude/sparse-nullspace 6374b78a, off e7755e56): the null space visits only the rows that may not be 0 in a column, with the same pivots and sums in the same order. Proof: a random test, old against new bit for bit, with ties, repeated rows and -0; the same bits end to end on the sample's drags, 19 sets and 113 functions. Timed pairs, old and new in one process: the 9397's steering no gain, the 42083's steering 11% less in the null space itself and 3% in a call, the 8110's function 8 no gain. The estimate of decision 277 was wrong because the time is in the elimination's updates, the fill (85.7 million in the 8110), which stage A does not touch. **Decided:** stage A is not merged on its own, since it adds code for very little; it is merged only if stage B builds on it. Stage B's count (how much less fill another order of elimination leaves, on the three cases) is the real test and comes after the mend of decision 278. Found on the way: a value that is not a number in the rows makes the null space loop without end, old and new, because the pivot search never settles. No model shows it, but a page that hangs is the worst failure a viewer has, so a guard goes in on a small branch of its own off preview: rows with a value that is not finite make the step fail as a refused step does, with a line in the console, and finite rows give the same bits, proved by the kernel tests.
281. **The test browsers draw with the Mac's graphics card; the load they made is gone.** Decision 273's trial by the worker (claude/gpu-browsers e1a30612, merged as aa91a1b6). A browser with no window drew WebGL in software and the viewer draws every frame, so one test browser took 3 to 4 cores for drawing, idle or not. Launched with `--use-angle=metal --enable-gpu --ignore-gpu-blocklist` (LAUNCH in `scripts/drags/drag.ts`, used by open(), the sweep and open-all; on a Mac only, and `KINEBRICK_SOFTWARE_GL=1` keeps software drawing), the drawing process takes 0.2 of a core idle and 0.02 in a drag, against 4.3 and 3.2. Software drawing had also starved the page: a drag of the 9397's steering did 15 steps in 20 s, and does 59 now. The worker's proof: the sweep on five functions of five kinds (a turn, a push, a ring, a lever as first step, a pointer drag) gives the same verdict, way, angle, moved parts and stops both ways, in 135 s against 1,282 s. My gate: the change read, the type check, and the 8285's function 5 swept on port 5174 with the same verdict in 5 s against 28 s, its drawing process at 0.5% of a core. So the plan to draw only when a test asks is dropped for now; what is left is the page's own 0.6 of a core idle from drawing each frame, which waits. The pool needs no count of threads: a browser is now at most about 1.2 cores, like any other process. Every session with a sweep or open-all running starts it again after merging preview. Found by my gate: the sweep tries only functions whose result is works, so a function held by decision 279 is never tried again and its hold could not go by itself; the sweep is to try a held function too, by what the check found (the worker's item 4).
282. **The 16 collision stops, a first sort: most are true contacts from a motion the real model does not make; two holds are lifted, and the count is 715.** The worker's probe (notes, viewer-sweep/stops16-probe.json; no code changed) turned each control from the built pose in steps of 0.01 rad and recorded the two parts that meet, their gap as built and how far they move against each other. (a) Seven are true contacts made by a wrong motion, so the collision test is right and the mechanism is wrong. In five a model that rolls on the ground swings or drags something the real model does not: the landing-gear legs of the 42113 (function 6) and the 8856 (function 8), a pin against a tyre as the 42106 rolls 115 LDU (function 2), and an axle that tips 105 degrees against the differential of the 8848 (functions 2 and 3). In the 42060 (function 7) turning a track sprocket swings a body 55 degrees, and in the 42069 (function 7) a door pushes two loose shock-absorber cylinders 10 LDU into the frame. The worker offered to check the five rolling functions lifted. Refused: the booklet says the model is pushed along, a lifted check would hide a motion the viewer shows, and the list says what the set does. They are the solver's fault of choosing a motion that swings free parts, the same class as the drift of free parts in decisions 272 and 278, and they go to the solver owner as that class's test cases. (b) Four are tight builds, a gap of 2 LDU or less that closes within a few degrees (42038 function 9, 42075 function 6, one way of the 8063's function 5, one way of the 42069's function 7). The real parts probably rub and pass. No mend, since any would be a tuned limit; they stay held and wait for a rule that is not one. (c) The 8063's function 5 is a changeover lever with a real end of travel (0.67 rad one way, to the axle beside the catch); the sweep judged it as a part that turns without end because the function's kind is a power take-off. Its hold was wrong and is lifted. The 8063's function 6 is stopped only in the sweep, at 1.5 rad, the very end of its turn; the check has no stop there and the probe turns 1.6 rad each way freely, so its hold rested on one reading against two and is lifted too. Both are checked again: 715 functions work, 239 partly. The 8844's function 4 also turns freely in the probe, but the check and my own sweep run both stop it, so its hold stays until the worker says whether the step size or the set-up makes the difference; a result must not depend on the step size (decision 262). (d) Four wait for the booklet or a closer look: 42031 function 2 (the boom may need raising first), 42040 function 3 (the hatch's pivot may be wrong), and 42100 function 9 and 42122 function 6, whose control has no joint of its own. **Ordered:** the worker's rule for the check (decision 279, item 2) judges a stop only where the stopped control is itself the part that turns without end, a wheel, a sprocket or a shaft, never by the function's kind alone, and the sweep's judge() gets the same mend, so that a lever in a power take-off function is not taken for a shaft. Then the sweep work, then the four of (d) with the booklet.
283. **The check's rule for a stop is coded but goes by the function's kind; it is changed before any proof, and its proof waits for Erik's word in the worker's session.** claude/stop-judge 6c196cbe (from 73aa16e4, so written before decision 282 reached the worker): a function whose kinds all turn without end and which a collision stopped is at most partly, with a new cause, stopped-endless; the kinds are shared with the sweep's judge() in `scripts/endless.ts`, and a kind with a note after a colon now counts as its kind. From the stored records the worker predicts: 29 functions in 19 sets have such a stop; 9 are held today and would come out partly by themselves, 14 that are works today would drop to partly, 6 are partly already. The 9 include the 8063's function 5, the lever, which decision 282 says must stay works, so the rule as coded is wrong there. **Decided:** (a) the rule goes by the stopped control being itself the part that turns without end, told from a lever by what the data already holds, with no new threshold; a function with some ways stopped is at most partly; (b) before any run the worker sends a table of the 29 from the records, with the stopped control, the pair, the angle and the result by the new test, so that I see the 14 before they drop; (c) that a stop may be a false contact does not change the rule, since the viewer stops there too; whether it is false is asked of each afterwards, as in decision 282. The worker's session was refused the run of the check by name by its permission check. I do not run for a session what it was refused, and no session looks for a way round it: the proof waits for Erik's word in that session, and it is on the decisions page as waiting for him.
284. **The stop rule's test is accepted: a stop counts where the stopped control is itself a wheel, gear, shaft or the like, by its role or else its LDraw title. A ring that a catch holds is the catch's to move; that is not a fault.** (a) The rule as changed (claude/stop-judge 3ca6bcfe, `endlessPart` in `scripts/endless.ts`): the stopped control must be a part that turns without end, by its role in `data/roles.json` (tyre, rim, gear, axle, differential, crankshaft, worm, train wheel), else by its LDraw title (wheel, rim, tyre, gear, sprocket, pulley, propeller, rotor, axle, worm, crankshaft, differential; not steering, hub, spindle, joiner, connector, towball), and the function's kinds must all turn without end as before. The joint cannot tell a wheel from a lever, since both turn on a revolute joint. A list of words in a title is a judgement and not a measure; it is accepted because the roles are too thin today (most rims have none) and it is no threshold, and the roles take over as the catalog walk of the project's plan fills them in. One judged stop among several controls or ways makes the function at most partly. From the stored records, with no run: of 366 functions with a collision stop, 22 would go from works to partly. Eight are held today, so their holds would no longer be needed (42038 function 9, 42060 function 7, 42106 function 2, 42113 function 6, 8844 function 4, 8848 functions 2 and 3, 8856 function 8). Fourteen are new: 42024 function 5, 42081 function 8, 42093 function 3, 42106 function 5, 42112 function 2, 42123 function 4, 8840 functions 2 and 5, 8844 function 2, 8848 function 7, 8849 functions 4 and 7, 8851 function 6, 9393 function 3. I read four of them in the records: the 42123's third wheel and all four sprockets of the 8851 stop at 0.1 rad each way, the 42081's fans at 0.07 to 0.73, the 8844's rotor drive at 0.37; each passed because the check took the stop for an end of travel. The 8063's function 5 stays works, its control being a connector. So the count would be 701 once the rule is proven. The proof, the check by name on the 17 sets, still waits for Erik's word in the worker's session (decision 283). (b) The worker's finding on the ten functions whose first step slides a driving ring: in the viewer a ring that a changeover catch holds goes back to the catch's fork when the drag ends, so a user moves it by turning the catch, as on the real model by its lever; the check slides the ring and keeps it. The sweep now turns the catch until the ring shifts, and on the 42038 the drive then turns. Decided: this is not a fault that holds a function, since the viewer shows the function by the control the real model has. Two things are noted for later, neither urgent: a ring pulled by hand should turn its catch along, as the real parts do, and the check's first step should move such a ring by its catch, so that the check does what a user can do.
285. **The working set's fault of decision 278 is mended and merged: the set threw away the motion kept for the next step.** The solver owner's finding (claude/working-set-strays a3f00c6b, merged as a6ebe8d3): the full calculation starts each turn from the motion the last turn kept for its next step; the working set dropped that kept motion on its first turn, and on a full turn after a short one, and worked the motion out afresh. A fresh motion differs from the kept one in the last bits, and where a drag's motion lies between two near choices at every step (the 42020's wheels, rolling against scrubbing) those bits sent the drag another way, so the turn back could not undo it. No column outside the set ever moved, so my guess in decision 278 was wrong. The mend removes three lines and adds no limit: the set takes the kept motion as the full calculation does. Proof by the solver owner: on the standing sample and the seven functions, 120 functions with the set on and off, no result changes; 83 are the same to the last figure, 37 differ in figures only, and 2 differ in a mark (the 42066's function 2 and the 8274's function 2, both functions that already end hundreds of LDU from their start, where a difference of one part in a million a turn is magnified). The 9397's steering keeps the set's gain: 18.9 ms a call against 215.9 ms with the set off, in one process. The 42106's functions 2 and 5 still stop one step earlier at their collision, because the set, working on 16 free motions and not 27, finds a rolling that scrubs a little less; that answer is different, not worse. The like pistons in another order come from the tie of decision 276, not from the set. My gate: the change read, the type check, all 1,074 tests, the 42020's function 2 with the set on and off (the same figures, no part out of place), and the check of the 42020, the 8274, the 42106 and the 42066, whose records are stored: three functions better (the three of decision 278, no way left not back), none worse, 715 work. `tests/keep-ahead.test.ts` now runs with the set off, since its claim (decision 174) is about the solver itself. Still open from decision 272: the full calculation's own drift of free parts, with the seven test cases of decision 282.
286. **The sweep's second pass: nine of the ten ring functions are shown, three more functions are held, and a result is found to depend on how often a drag chooses its holds.** The worker's item 4 (claude/sweep-steps 8842c0d6, records 944a9cae; not merged yet, because the branch carries the stop rule that waits for its proof, decision 283). The sweep now moves a ring by its catch, does a slide as a first step with Shift and a push, looks for a control from 72 views, and tries held functions by what the check found. (a) Of the ten functions whose first step slides a ring, nine are shown by turn and pointer both (42038 functions 2 and 3, 42039 functions 8 and 9, 42054 function 4, 42112 function 6, 8110 function 8, 8258 function 5, 9397 function 2); the 42054's function 3 is shown by running its motor, which is how a user works it. They are no longer doubtful. (b) The 8285's function 5: its control is now seen and neither a turn nor a push moves the listed part; the check itself tilts that bonnet only 0.15 rad. Held. (c) The 42043's function 10 and the 42075's function 2 (pushing the model runs the engine): in one long turn of a wheel in the viewer the drive slips, the 42043's pistons hardly move and the 42075's crankshaft turns 25.8 degrees for 86 in where the rate is 3.89, while four short drags by pointer move them. A user who pushes the model far sees the engine stand still, so decision 267's rule, that the pointer alone is enough, is narrowed: where one long turn in the viewer moves the listed parts less than the check's rate says, the function is held, whatever short drags do. Both are held. The 8063's function 8 and the 8419's function 3 fail in the turn only because the check turns them 0.23 rad, too little to measure; they stay shown. (d) The cause of (c), and of the 8844's function 4: the worker tried that wheel with the holds chosen once for the turn and chosen afresh before each small call. Chosen once, it stops at 0.19 to 0.21 rad at parts 36 and 215, at any step size; chosen afresh each call, it turns 1.5 rad each way with no stop. So the result does not depend on the step size (decision 262 holds) but on how often the holds are chosen, and the viewer's drag chooses once. A result must not depend on that either. This goes to the solver owner as a class of its own, with the three functions as test cases; its hold stays. (e) Not settled: the 42078's function 12 (the viewer refuses the slide of the lock bars that the check makes; the worker traces whether the check frees pins first), and the two controls no view shows, the 42078's function 4 (a bonnet that the check tips only 0.02 and 0.03 rad) and the 42080's function 1 (a motor's pump crank that the check turns 0.3 and 0.39 rad). These two and the 8285's bonnet are of the kind the booklet decides (decision 252: a travel of 0.2 rad or so where the picture shows far), and they join the worker's booklet item; for the 42080 the control should probably be the motor, a list change to offer. The count is 712 when the check has written the three sets' records.
287. **Stage B is counted: the rows go into the null space sparse now, with the same bits; the other elimination is not built yet.** The solver owner's count on f4b71f03 (a scratch harness that records every null space a full step asks for, and counts the present Gauss-Jordan elimination against an LU with Markowitz pivoting): the elimination's updates fall from 5.22 million to 0.36 million on the 9397's steering, 6.12 to 0.45 on the 42083's steering, and 111.1 to 2.6 on the 8110's function 8. A first version that chose pivots by a column threshold alone got the rank wrong in 4 of the 9397's 13 null spaces, taking a pivot of 2.1e-6 just above the rank tolerance where the present elimination leaves 2.3e-16; with threshold rook pivoting (a pivot at least 0.1 of the largest in its row and its column) every rank agrees, 68 of 68, and the residuals are the same. But a rough prototype gains time only on the 8110 (674 ms against 1,787 ms for all its null spaces); on the 42083 and the 9397 it gains nothing (444 against 413, 128 against 127), because their time is in the rows themselves, which come in dense (11.45 million cells on the 42083, 1.4% of them not 0) and are copied and passed over once.
